I mean, I know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under those restaurant lights. It makes me feel like I look old, or strange, and it gets to me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly the hairline and my face, yeah. Especially at work, under the restaurant lights. I keep thinking it makes me look old or… strange. Other people say they don’t see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face are realistic, or might they be a bit worse in your mind than they actually are?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows my coworkers say they don’t see it. So maybe it’s worse in my head.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m under those lights. I just keep seeing my hairline and my face looking old or strange, and I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face affected your ability to work or interact with others at your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’ve been late or missed shifts because of it, and I got written up at the restaurant.\n\nI also kind of avoid things now. Staff photos, gallery events, hikes… stuff where I feel like people are going to see me that way. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your appearance changed over time, or have they always been about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly been this, lately. My hairline, and how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nI just get stuck on it, like I look old or strange, even when people at work tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or had difficulty interacting with friends or family because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make yourself look better or to check how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nBefore a shift I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, checking it. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at work. I feel like it makes me look old, kind of strange. People at work say they don’t notice it, but… I do.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad. I mean, I know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under those restaurant lights. It makes me feel like I look old, or strange, and it gets to me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly the hairline and my face, yeah. Especially at work, under the restaurant lights. I keep thinking it makes me look old or… strange. Other people say they don’t see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face are realistic, or might they be a bit worse in your mind than they actually are?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows my coworkers say they don’t see it. I just keep seeing my hairline and my face looking old or strange, and I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face affected your ability to work or interact with others at your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. Staff photos, gallery events, hikes… stuff where I feel like people are going to see me that way. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your appearance changed over time, or have they always been about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly been this, lately. My hairline, and how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nI just get stuck on it, like I look old or strange, even when people at work tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or had difficulty interacting with friends or family because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make yourself look better or to check how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nBefore a shift I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, checking it. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I feel like it makes me look old, kind of strange. People at work say they don’t notice it, but… I do.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad. I mean, I know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under those restaurant lights. It makes me feel like I look old, or strange, and it gets to me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly the hairline and my face, yeah. Especially at work, under the restaurant lights. I keep thinking it makes me look old or… strange. Other people say they don’t see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face are realistic, or might they be a bit worse in your mind than they actually are?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows my coworkers say they don’t see it. So maybe it’s worse in my head.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m under those lights. I just keep seeing my hairline and my face looking old or strange, and I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face affected your ability to work or interact with others at your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’ve been late or missed shifts because of it, and I got written up at the restaurant.\n\nI also kind of avoid things now. Staff photos, gallery events, hikes… stuff where I feel like people are going to see me that way. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your appearance changed over time, or have they always been about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly been this, lately. My hairline, and how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nI just get stuck on it, like I look old or strange, even when people at work tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or had difficulty interacting with friends or family because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make yourself look better or to check how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nBefore a shift I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, checking it. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. Staff photos, gallery things, hikes. I avoid it because I can’t stand being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too — I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see you as old or strange when they look at you, or is it just that you feel that way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I don’t really know what they actually see.\n\nCoworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but when I’m under the restaurant lights, all I can think is that my hairline and my face make me look old. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. Staff photos, gallery things, hikes. I avoid it because I can’t stand being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too — I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see you as old or strange when they look at you, or is it just that you feel that way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I don’t really know what they actually see.\n\nCoworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but when I’m under the restaurant lights, all I can think is that my hairline and my face make me look old. Strange, maybe. It feels obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look deformed or disfigured when you see yourself in the mirror or in photos?\n\nPatient: Deformed… I don’t know if I’d use that word.\n\nBut in the mirror or in photos, especially with those restaurant lights, I see my hairline and my face and I just look… old. Strange. It’s hard to believe other people don’t see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at work. I feel like it makes me look old, kind of strange. People at work say they don’t notice it, but… I do.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad. I mean, I know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under those restaurant lights. It makes me feel like I look old, or strange, and it gets to me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly the hairline and my face, yeah. Especially at work, under the restaurant lights. I keep thinking it makes me look old or… strange. Other people say they don’t see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face are realistic, or might they be a bit worse in your mind than they actually are?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows my coworkers say they don’t see it. So maybe it’s worse in my head.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m under those lights. I just keep seeing my hairline and my face looking old or strange, and I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face affected your ability to work or interact with others at your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’ve been late or missed shifts because of it, and I got written up at the restaurant.\n\nI also kind of avoid things now. Staff photos, gallery events, hikes… stuff where I feel like people are going to see me that way. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your appearance changed over time, or have they always been about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly been this, lately. My hairline, and how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nI just get stuck on it, like I look old or strange, even when people at work tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or had difficulty interacting with friends or family because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make yourself look better or to check how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nBefore a shift I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, checking it. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. Staff photos, gallery things, hikes. I avoid it because I can’t stand being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too — I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see you as old or strange when they look at you, or is it just that you feel that way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I don’t really know what they actually see.\n\nCoworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but when I’m under the restaurant lights, all I can think is that my hairline and my face make me look old. Strange, maybe. It feels obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look deformed or disfigured when you see yourself in the mirror or in photos?\n\nPatient: Deformed… I don’t know if I’d use that word.\n\nBut in the mirror or in photos, especially with those restaurant lights, I see my hairline and my face and I just look… old. Strange. It’s hard to believe other people don’t see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance made it difficult for you to maintain relationships or achieve your goals outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to say yes to — staff photos, gallery events, hikes. I just can’t handle being seen that way. And at the restaurant it’s… affected things. I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push these thoughts about your hairline and face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Once it starts, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nI keep thinking about my hairline, and how my face looks under those lights… like I look old or strange. Even if people at work say they don’t see it, I can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at work. I feel like it makes me look old, kind of strange. People at work say they don’t notice it, but… I do.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your appearance cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad. I mean, I know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under those restaurant lights. It makes me feel like I look old, or strange, and it gets to me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly the hairline and my face, yeah. Especially at work, under the restaurant lights. I keep thinking it makes me look old or… strange. Other people say they don’t see it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face are realistic, or might they be a bit worse in your mind than they actually are?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows my coworkers say they don’t see it. So maybe it’s worse in my head.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way when I’m under those lights. I just keep seeing my hairline and my face looking old or strange, and I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your hairline and face affected your ability to work or interact with others at your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’ve been late or missed shifts because of it, and I got written up at the restaurant.\n\nI also kind of avoid things now. Staff photos, gallery events, hikes… stuff where I feel like people are going to see me that way. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your appearance changed over time, or have they always been about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly been this, lately. My hairline, and how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nI just get stuck on it, like I look old or strange, even when people at work tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or had difficulty interacting with friends or family because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make yourself look better or to check how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nBefore a shift I’ll keep going back to the bathroom mirror, checking it. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. Staff photos, gallery things, hikes. I avoid it because I can’t stand being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too — I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see you as old or strange when they look at you, or is it just that you feel that way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I don’t really know what they actually see.\n\nCoworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but when I’m under the restaurant lights, all I can think is that my hairline and my face make me look old. Strange, maybe. It feels obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look deformed or disfigured when you see yourself in the mirror or in photos?\n\nPatient: Deformed… I don’t know if I’d use that word.\n\nBut in the mirror or in photos, especially with those restaurant lights, I see my hairline and my face and I just look… old. Strange. I don’t really like being seen right now.\n\nI’ve skipped things like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… and at work I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Sometimes I take pictures on my phone from different angles, try to comb my hair forward… just to see if it looks less noticeable.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there thinking about how the guests are going to see me, and I end up late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your appearance in the mirror or take pictures of yourself a certain number of times, or is it more that you just can't help yourself and do it as often as you think about it?\n\nPatient: It’s not like a number I have to hit, no.\n\nIt’s more… once I think about it, I can’t really leave it alone. I’ll check the bathroom mirror again, take another picture from a different angle, mess with my hair, and then I’m thinking about how people out front are going to see me. Sometimes it just keeps going until I’m late or I can’t make myself go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided or ended any romantic relationships because of your concerns about your hairline and face?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really want to get into dating stuff.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been avoiding being around people in general. Like at the restaurant, I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up. And I stopped going to staff photos, gallery events, hikes… things where I’d have to be seen that way.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your hairline and face ever changed to focusing on a different part of your body?\n\nPatient: Not really. It’s mostly stayed on my hairline and my face… especially how they look under the lights at work.\n\nI know people say they don’t see it, but I can’t stop thinking I look old or strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your appearance is something you can control, or is it something that you feel forced to do?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel very controlled.\n\nI tell myself I’ll just check once, but then I’m back in the bathroom mirror, taking another picture, combing my hair forward… trying to see what guests are going to see. It can go on until I’m late, or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your concerns about how you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’ve “lost” friends exactly.\n\nI’ve just… stopped showing up. Staff photos, gallery things, hikes. I avoid it because I can’t stand being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too — I’ve been late or missed shifts and got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see you as old or strange when they look at you, or is it just that you feel that way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I don’t really know what they actually see.\n\nCoworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but when I’m under the restaurant lights, all I can think is that my hairline and my face make me look old. Strange, maybe. It feels obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look deformed or disfigured when you see yourself in the mirror or in photos?\n\nPatient: Deformed… I don’t know if I’d use that word.\n\nBut in the mirror or in photos, especially with those restaurant lights, I see my hairline and my face and I just look… old. Strange. It’s hard to believe other people don’t see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance made it difficult for you to maintain relationships or achieve your goals outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to say yes to — staff photos, gallery events, hikes. I just can’t handle being seen that way. And at the restaurant it’s… affected things. I’ve been late or missed shifts enough that I got written up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to push these thoughts about your hairline and face out of your mind once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Once it starts, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nI keep thinking about my hairline, and how my face looks under those lights… like I look old or strange. Even if people at work say they don’t see it, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your hairline and face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is.\n\nI mean, I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts. And I don’t really go to things anymore — staff photos, gallery events, hikes. I just… don’t want to be seen like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look so bad that people can't help but notice it when they see you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know people say they don’t notice it, but under the restaurant lights I feel like my hairline and my face just… make me look old. Strange. Like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or rejecting you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do worry they’re looking at it.\n\nIt’s more that I can’t stop thinking my hairline, and my face under those restaurant lights, makes me look old or strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like they must notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
